<div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Name traditionally given to the author of the Gospel of John</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1066479718">.mw-parser-output .infobox-subbox{padding:0;border:none;margin:-3px;width:auto;min-width:100%;font-size:100%;clear:none;float:none;background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .infobox-3cols-child{margin:auto}.mw-parser-output .infobox .navbar{font-size:100%}body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-header,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-subheader,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-above,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-title,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-image,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-full-data,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-below{text-align:center}</style><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div class="honorific-prefix" style="display:inline;font-size: 77%; <p><b> John the Evangelist</b> (<a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Ἰωάννης</span></span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek" title="Romanization of Greek">translit.</a></small>&#160;<span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language romanization"><i lang="grc-Latn"><i>Iōánnēs</i></i></span>; <a href="/wiki/Aramaic" title="Aramaic">Aramaic</a>: ܝܘܚܢܢ; <a href="/wiki/Ge%27ez" class="mw-redirect" title="Ge&#39;ez">Ge'ez</a>: ዮሐንስ; <a href="/wiki/Arabic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabic language">Arabic</a>: <span lang="ar" dir="rtl">يوحنا الإنجيلي</span>, <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Ioannes</i>, <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a>: <span lang="he" dir="rtl">יוחנן</span> <a href="/wiki/Coptic_language" title="Coptic language">Coptic</a>: <span lang="cop">ⲓⲱⲁⲛⲛⲏⲥ or ⲓⲱ̅ⲁ</span><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>) is the name traditionally given to the author of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_John" title="Gospel of John">Gospel of John</a>. Christians have traditionally identified him with <a href="/wiki/John_the_Apostle" title="John the Apostle">John the Apostle</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_of_Patmos" title="John of Patmos">John of Patmos</a>, and <a href="/wiki/John_the_Presbyter" title="John the Presbyter">John the Presbyter</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Harris_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harris-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> although this has been disputed by most modern scholars.<sup id="cite_ref-Ehrman_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ehrman-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Identity">Identity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Identity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Johannine_literature" title="Johannine literature">Johannine literature</a> and <a href="/wiki/Four_Evangelists" title="Four Evangelists">Four Evangelists</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:276px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Johannes_(evangelist).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Johannes_%28evangelist%29.jpg/274px-Johannes_%28evangelist%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="274" height="272" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Johannes_%28evangelist%29.jpg/411px-Johannes_%28evangelist%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Johannes_%28evangelist%29.jpg/548px-Johannes_%28evangelist%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2456" data-file-height="2442" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Johannes_(evangelist).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Print of John the Evangelist. Preserved in the <a href="/wiki/Ghent_University_Library" title="Ghent University Library">Ghent University Library</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>The Gospel of John refers to an otherwise unnamed "<a href="/wiki/Disciple_whom_Jesus_loved" title="Disciple whom Jesus loved">disciple whom Jesus loved</a>", who "bore witness to and wrote" the Gospel's message.<sup id="cite_ref-TM1998_2_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TM1998_2-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> The author of the Gospel of John seemed interested in maintaining the internal anonymity of the author's identity, although interpreting the Gospel in the light of the <a href="/wiki/Synoptic_Gospel" class="mw-redirect" title="Synoptic Gospel">Synoptic Gospels</a> and considering that the author names (and therefore is not claiming to be) Peter, and that James was martyred as early as AD 44,<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> it has been widely believed that the author was the Apostle John (though some believe he was <a href="/wiki/Pseudepigrapha" title="Pseudepigrapha">pretending to be</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-TM1998_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TM1998-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Christian_tradition" title="Christian tradition">Christian tradition</a> says that John the Evangelist was <a href="/wiki/John_the_Apostle" title="John the Apostle">John the Apostle</a>. The Apostle John was one of the "pillars" of the <a href="/wiki/Early_centers_of_Christianity#Jerusalem" class="mw-redirect" title="Early centers of Christianity">Jerusalem church</a> after Jesus' death.<sup id="cite_ref-Harris_John_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harris_John-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> He was one of the original <a href="/wiki/Twelve_apostles" class="mw-redirect" title="Twelve apostles">twelve apostles</a> and is thought to be the only one to have not been killed for his faith. It had been believed that he was exiled (around AD 95) to the Aegean island of <a href="/wiki/Patmos" title="Patmos">Patmos</a>, where he wrote the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Revelation" title="Book of Revelation">Book of Revelation</a>. However, some attribute the authorship of Revelation to another man, called <a href="/wiki/John_the_Presbyter" title="John the Presbyter">John the Presbyter</a>, or to other writers of the late first century AD.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Bauckham argues that the early Christians identified John the Evangelist with <a href="/wiki/John_the_Presbyter" title="John the Presbyter">John the Presbyter</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Bauckham2007_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bauckham2007-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Authorship_of_the_Johannine_works">Authorship of the Johannine works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Authorship of the Johannine works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Authorship_of_the_Johannine_works" title="Authorship of the Johannine works">Authorship of the Johannine works</a> and <a href="/wiki/Johannine_epistles" title="Johannine epistles">Johannine epistles</a></div>
<p>The authorship of the <a href="/wiki/Johannine_works" class="mw-redirect" title="Johannine works">Johannine works</a> has been debated by <a href="/wiki/Biblical_criticism" title="Biblical criticism">scholars</a> since at least the 2nd century AD.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> The main debate centers on who authored the writings, and which of the writings, if any, can be ascribed to a common author.
</p><p>Eastern Orthodox tradition attributes all of the Johannine books to John the Apostle.<sup id="cite_ref-Harris_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harris-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 6th century, the <i><a href="/wiki/Decretum_Gelasianum" class="mw-redirect" title="Decretum Gelasianum">Decretum Gelasianum</a></i> argued that <a href="/wiki/Second_Epistle_of_John" title="Second Epistle of John">Second</a> and <a href="/wiki/Third_Epistle_of_John" title="Third Epistle of John">Third John</a> have a separate author known as "John, a priest" (see <a href="/wiki/John_the_Presbyter" title="John the Presbyter">John the Presbyter</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Historical_criticism" title="Historical criticism">Historical critics</a>, like H.P.V. Nunn,<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> the non-Christians <a href="/wiki/Reza_Aslan" title="Reza Aslan">Reza Aslan</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Bart_Ehrman" class="mw-redirect" title="Bart Ehrman">Bart Ehrman</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> reject the view that John the Apostle authored any of these works.
</p><p>Most modern scholars believe that the apostle John wrote none of these works,<sup id="cite_ref-Fourth_Gospel_1985_p._355_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fourth_Gospel_1985_p._355-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Kelly2012_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kelly2012-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> although some, such as <a href="/wiki/John_Arthur_Thomas_Robinson" class="mw-redirect" title="John Arthur Thomas Robinson">J.A.T. Robinson</a>, <a href="/wiki/F._F._Bruce" title="F. F. Bruce">F. F. Bruce</a>, <a href="/wiki/Leon_Morris" title="Leon Morris">Leon Morris</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Martin_Hengel" title="Martin Hengel">Martin Hengel</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> hold the apostle to be behind at least some, in particular the gospel.<sup id="cite_ref-Morris1995_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Morris1995-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Strobel,_Lee_1998_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Strobel,_Lee_1998-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There may have been a single author for the gospel and the three epistles.<sup id="cite_ref-Harris_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harris-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Some scholars conclude the author of the epistles was different from that of the gospel, although all four works originated from the same community.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The gospel and epistles traditionally and plausibly came from <a href="/wiki/Early_centers_of_Christianity#Asia_Minor" class="mw-redirect" title="Early centers of Christianity">Ephesus</a>, <i>c.</i> 90–110, although some scholars argue for an origin in <a href="/wiki/Early_centers_of_Christianity#Damascus" class="mw-redirect" title="Early centers of Christianity">Syria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the case of Revelation, most modern scholars agree that it was written by a separate author, <a href="/wiki/John_of_Patmos" title="John of Patmos">John of Patmos</a>, <i>c.</i> 95 with some parts possibly dating to <a href="/wiki/Nero" title="Nero">Nero</a>'s reign in the early 60s.<sup id="cite_ref-Harris_2-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harris-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Ehrman_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ehrman-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Feast_day">Feast day</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Feast day">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> of Saint John in the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>, <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a>, and the Lutheran Calendar, is on 27 December, the <a href="/wiki/Twelve_Days_of_Christmas" title="Twelve Days of Christmas">third day</a> of <a href="/wiki/Christmastide" title="Christmastide">Christmastide</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Dame2006_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dame2006-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/Tridentine_Calendar" class="mw-redirect" title="Tridentine Calendar">Tridentine Calendar</a> he was commemorated also on each of the following days up to and including 3 January, the <a href="/wiki/Octave_(liturgy)" title="Octave (liturgy)">Octave</a> of the 27 December feast. This Octave was abolished by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a> in 1955.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> The traditional liturgical color is <a href="/wiki/White" title="White">white</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Freemasonry" title="Freemasonry">Freemasons</a> <a href="/wiki/St._John%27s_Day,_Masonic_feast" title="St. John&#39;s Day, Masonic feast">celebrate this feast day</a>, dating back to the 18th century when the Feast Day was used for the installation of Presidents and Grand Masters.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_art">In art</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: In art">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>John is traditionally depicted in one of two distinct ways: either as an aged man with a white or gray beard, or alternatively as a beardless youth.<sup id="cite_ref-HallJerousek_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-HallJerousek-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-BritannicaOnline_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BritannicaOnline-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The first way of depicting him was more common in <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_art" title="Byzantine art">Byzantine art</a>, where it was possibly influenced by antique depictions of <a href="/wiki/Socrates" title="Socrates">Socrates</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> the second was more common in the art of <a href="/wiki/Medieval_art" title="Medieval art">Medieval Western Europe</a> and can be dated back as far as 4th-century Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-BritannicaOnline_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BritannicaOnline-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In medieval works of painting, sculpture and literature, Saint John is often presented in an androgynous or feminized manner.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Historians have related such portrayals to the circumstances of the believers for whom they were intended.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> For instance, John's feminine features are argued to have helped to make him more relatable to women.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Likewise, Sarah McNamer argues that because of John's androgynous status, he could function as an 'image of a third or mixed gender'<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> and 'a crucial figure with whom to identify'<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> for male believers who sought to cultivate an attitude of <a href="/wiki/Affective_piety" title="Affective piety">affective piety</a>, a highly emotional style of devotion that, in late-medieval culture, was thought to be poorly compatible with masculinity.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Legends from the "<a href="/wiki/Acts_of_John" title="Acts of John">Acts of John</a>" contributed much to medieval iconography; it is the source of the idea that John became an apostle at a young age.<sup id="cite_ref-BritannicaOnline_27-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BritannicaOnline-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> One of John's familiar attributes is the <a href="/wiki/Chalice" title="Chalice">chalice</a>, often with a snake emerging from it.<sup id="cite_ref-Hall1979_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hall1979-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> According to one legend from the Acts of John,<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> John was challenged to drink a cup of poison to demonstrate the power of his faith, and thanks to God's aid the poison was rendered harmless.<sup id="cite_ref-Hall1979_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hall1979-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> The chalice can also be interpreted with reference to the <a href="/wiki/Last_Supper" title="Last Supper">Last Supper</a>, or to the words of Christ to John and James: "My chalice indeed you shall drink."<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CathEnc_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CathEnc-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> According to the 1910 <i>Catholic Encyclopedia</i>, some authorities believe that this symbol was not adopted until the 13th century.<sup id="cite_ref-CathEnc_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CathEnc-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> There was also a legend that John was at some stage boiled in oil and miraculously preserved.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Another common attribute is a book or a scroll, in reference to his writings.<sup id="cite_ref-Hall1979_35-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hall1979-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> John the Evangelist is symbolically represented by an <a href="/wiki/Eagle" title="Eagle">eagle</a>, one of the creatures envisioned by Ezekiel (1:10) and in the Book of Revelation (4:7).<sup id="cite_ref-CathEnc_39-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CathEnc-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li id="cite_note-18"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-18">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFHengel2000" class="citation book cs1">Hengel, Martin (2000). <i>Four Gospels and the One Gospel of Jesus Christ, 1st edition</i>. Trinity Press International. p.&#160;40. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-56338-300-7" title="Special:BookSources/978-1-56338-300-7"><bdi>978-1-56338-300-7</bdi></a>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Four+Gospels+and+the+One+Gospel+of+Jesus+Christ%2C+1st+edition&amp;rft.pages=40&amp;rft.pub=Trinity+Press+International&amp;rft.date=2000&amp;rft.isbn=978-1-56338-300-7&amp;rft.aulast=Hengel&amp;rft.aufirst=Martin&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AJohn+the+Evangelist" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Morris1995-19"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Morris1995_19-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Morris, Leon (1995) <i><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=II-33dS9esAC&amp;pg=PA4#v=snippet&amp;q=Continental%20scholars&amp;f=false">The Gospel According to John</a></i> Volume 4 of The new international commentary on the New Testament, Wm. B. Eerdmans Publishing, <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-8028-2504-9" title="Special:BookSources/978-0-8028-2504-9">978-0-8028-2504-9</a>, pp. 4–5, 24, 35–7. "Continental scholars have&#160;... abandoned the idea that this gospel was written by the apostle John, whereas in Great Britain and America scholarship has been much more open to the idea." Abandonment is due to changing opinion rather "than to any new evidence." "Werner, Colson, and I have been joined, among others, by I. Howard Marshall and J.A.T. Robinson in seeing the evidence as pointing to John the son of Zebedee as the author of this Gospel." The view that John's history is substandard "is becoming increasingly hard to sustain. Many recent writers have shown that there is good reason for regarding this or that story in John as authentic.&#160;... It is difficult to&#160;... regard John as having little concern for history. The fact is John is concerned with historical information.&#160;... John apparently records this kind of information because he believes it to be accurate.&#160;... He has some reliable information and has recorded it carefully.&#160;... The evidence is that where he can be tested John proves to be remarkably accurate."
<ul><li>Bruce 1981 pp. 52–4, 58. "The evidence&#160;... favor[s] the apostolicity of the gospel.&#160;... John knew the other gospels and&#160;... supplements them.&#160;... The synoptic narrative becomes more intelligible if we follow John." John's style is different so Jesus' "abiding truth might be presented to men and women who were quite unfamiliar with the original setting.&#160;... He does not yield to any temptation to restate Christianity.&#160;... It is the story of events that happened in history.&#160;... John does not divorce the story from its Palestinian context."</li>
<li>Dodd p. 444. "Revelation is distinctly, and nowhere more clearly than in the Fourth Gospel, a historical revelation. It follows that it is important for the evangelist that what he narrates happened."</li>
<li>Temple, William. "Readings in St. John's Gospel". MacMillan and Co, 1952. "The synoptists give us something more like the perfect photograph; St. John gives us the more perfect portrait".</li>
<li>Edwards, R. A. "The Gospel According to St. John" 1954, p 9. One reason he accepts John's authorship is because "the alternative solutions seem far too complicated to be possible in a world where living men met and talked".</li>
<li>Hunter, A. M. "Interpreting the New Testament" P 86. "After all the conjectures have been heard, the likeliest view is that which identifies the Beloved Disciple with the Apostle John.</li></ul>
</span></li>
<li id="cite_note-Strobel,_Lee_1998-20"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Strobel,_Lee_1998_20-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Dr. <a href="/wiki/Craig_Blomberg" title="Craig Blomberg">Craig Blomberg</a>, cited in Lee Strobel <i>The Case for Christ</i>, 1998, Chapter 2.
<ul><li>Marshall, Howard. "The Illustrated Bible Dictionary", ed J. D. Douglas et al. Leicester 1980. II, p 804</li>
<li>Robinson, J. A. T. "The Priority of John" P 122</li>
<li>Cf. Marsh, "John seems to have believed that theology was not something which could be used to read a meaning into events but rather something that was to be discovered in them. His story is what it is because his theology is what it is; but his theology is what it is because the story happened so" (p&#160;580–581).</li></ul>
</span></li>

<li id="cite_note-28"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-28">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">
Jadranka Prolović, "Socrates and St. John the Apostle: the interchangеable similarity of their portraits" <i>Zograf,</i> vol. 35 (2011), 9: "It is difficult to locate when and where this iconography of John originated and what the prototype was, yet it is clearly visible that this iconography of John contains all of the main characteristics of well-known antique images of Socrates. This fact leads to the conclusion that Byzantine artists used depictions of Socrates as a model for the portrait of John."</span>
